People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Yeah, um… I came in because my sleep has just kind of fallen apart. I’ll be in bed in my apartment for hours, like I’ve already closed my laptop and I’m technically “done” for the night, but my brain is still going through work deadlines and tuition payments and class assignments, like I’m checking them over and over in my head.

And then even when I do sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really turned off. I don’t know, it’s like my body was asleep but my mind was still working the whole time.

But yes, it’s everyday stuff too.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, though it’s less like I’m avoiding one specific place and more like I’ve just started dropping things. I keep meaning to catch up, but then I’ll spend forever trying to figure out the “right” order to do things in, and suddenly the night is gone.\n\nI also stopped doing the stuff that used to keep me kind of steady—yoga, running, cooking actual meals. keeps jumping from whether I’m falling behind at work, to whether I’m wasting money on school, to whether my family will think I can’t handle things, and I treat every small issue like it could derail my whole future.\n- key: S057\n  name: Uncontrollable_Worry\n  description: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.\n  manifestation: I try to tell myself to I mean, that’s where it starts a lot of the time, but then my mind jumps around. Then I’ll try to make a list to calm down, but then the list becomes its own whole thing, and I’m color-coding it, and—sorry, I know that’s not really the point. But yes, it’s everyday stuff too.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, though it’s less like I’m avoiding one specific place and more like I’ve just started dropping things. I keep meaning to catch up, but then I’ll spend forever trying to figure out the “right” order to do things in, and suddenly the night is gone.\n\nI also stopped doing the stuff that used to keep me kind of steady—yoga, running, cooking actual meals. I… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say sad exactly, or at least I haven’t really stopped to label it that way. Less interest—maybe? But it’s hard to tell because everything feels tangled up with just being behind and tired.

Like, I used to enjoy cooking, but then if I think about cooking now I immediately think about groceries, and money, and whether I bought the right things, and then I remember this one time I made this really good lentil soup—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.

I guess I haven’t noticed a clear “I don’t enjoy anything anymore” feeling. It’s more that I don’t get to things, or I overthink them before I even start.

I used to be weirdly proud of my Sunday meal prep containers, like all lined up in the fridge, and now I just… don’t do it. It’s frustrating because I used to be able to push through a long day better than this.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or having less interest in things you used to enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I’m not sure. I mean, I guess everyone thinks about that sometimes, right? I’ll be in bed in my apartment for hours, like I’ve already closed my laptop and I’m technically “done” for the night, but my brain is still going through work deadlines and tuition payments and class assignments, like I’m checking them over and over in my head.\n\nAnd then even when I do sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really turned off. I don’t know, it’s like my body was asleep but my mind was still working the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you think might have started these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was one single dramatic thing, exactly. It’s more like… it started around when I began my evening graduate certificate program, while I was still working full-time as an analyst. That was about a year and three months ago.\n\nI remember telling myself it would be manageable because classes were in the evening, and I had this whole spreadsheet for work deadlines and tuition and assignments—actually, I make spreadsheets for everything, even groceries sometimes—but yeah, after that started, the sleep problems just kind of became my normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things, not just work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely not just work or school. I mean, that’s where it starts a lot of the time, but then my mind jumps around. Like, I’ll worry I’m falling behind at work, then suddenly I’m thinking maybe I’m wasting all this money on school, and then somehow that turns into, “What if my family thinks I can’t handle my life?”\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it can be some small thing, like one assignment or one bill or even just an email I haven’t answered, and my brain treats it like it’s going to mess up my entire future. Then I’ll try to make a list to calm down, but then the list becomes its own whole thing, and I’m color-coding it, and—sorry, I know that’s not really the point. But yes, it’s everyday stuff too.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, though it’s less like I’m avoiding one specific place and more like I’ve just started dropping things. Like, I’ll put off opening certain work files because I’m worried I already messed something up, and then that turns into missing deadlines, which is… not great.\n\nAnd with my certificate classes, I’m behind now too. I keep meaning to catch up, but then I’ll spend forever trying to figure out the “right” order to do things in, and suddenly the night is gone.\n\nI also stopped doing the stuff that used to keep me kind of steady—yoga, running, cooking actual meals. I used to be weirdly proud of my Sunday meal prep containers, like all lined up in the fridge, and now I just… don’t do it. So, yes, I think my worries are making me avoid things, or maybe making everything feel too big to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel extremely tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Yes. By midafternoon at the office I feel like I’ve been unplugged or something. Even answering normal emails feels like this huge task, and sometimes walking to the subway after work feels weirdly hard, like I’m dragging myself.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll just recover this weekend,” but the weekend comes and I rest, or I try to rest, and by Monday I don’t feel like I got my energy back. It’s frustrating because I used to be able to push through a long day better than this.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or having less interest in things you used to enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say sad exactly, or at least I haven’t really stopped to label it that way. Less interest—maybe? But it’s hard to tell because everything feels tangled up with just being behind and tired.\n\nLike, I used to enjoy cooking, but then if I think about cooking now I immediately think about groceries, and money, and whether I bought the right things, and then I remember this one time I made this really good lentil soup—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed a clear “I don’t enjoy anything anymore” feeling. It’s more that I don’t get to things, or I overthink them before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about what other people think of you in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I guess everyone thinks about that sometimes, right? Like if I’m in a meeting and I say something a little awkward, I might replay it later, but then again I also replay emails and, honestly, things like whether I forgot to move my laundry to the dryer, which is not the same thing.\n\nSocial situations… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. Sometimes I’m more focused on just getting through the thing or not seeming scattered, but I can’t tell if that’s about what people think of me specifically or just… everything feeling like it has too many moving parts.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, with other people… sometimes I’m thinking about what to say next, or whether I’m being normal, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nIt also depends who it is. If it’s coworkers, there’s all this meeting stuff and Slack messages and—sorry, I’m drifting. I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern of feeling anxious just because I’m with people. It’s more like I’m already wound up, and then being around people can feel like one more thing to manage.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because my sleep has just kind of fallen apart. I’ll be in bed in my apartment for hours, like I’ve already closed my laptop and I’m technically “done” for the night, but my brain is still going through work deadlines and tuition payments and class assignments, like I’m checking them over and over in my head.\n\nAnd then even when I do sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really turned off. I don’t know, it’s like my body was asleep but my mind was still working the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you think might have started these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was one single dramatic thing, exactly. It’s more like… it started around when I began my evening graduate certificate program, while I was still working full-time as an analyst. That was about a year and three months ago.\n\nI remember telling myself it would be manageable because classes were in the evening, and I had this whole spreadsheet for work deadlines and tuition and assignments—actually, I make spreadsheets for everything, even groceries sometimes—but yeah, after that started, the sleep problems just kind of became my normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things, not just work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely not just work or school. I mean, that’s where it starts a lot of the time, but then my mind jumps around. Like, I’ll worry I’m falling behind at work, then suddenly I’m thinking maybe I’m wasting all this money on school, and then somehow that turns into, “What if my family thinks I can’t handle my life?”\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it can be some small thing, like one assignment or one bill or even just an email I haven’t answered, and my brain treats it like it’s going to mess up my entire future. Then I’ll try to make a list to calm down, but then the list becomes its own whole thing, and I’m color-coding it, and—sorry, I know that’s not really the point. But yes, it’s everyday stuff too.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, though it’s less like I’m avoiding one specific place and more like I’ve just started dropping things. Like, I’ll put off opening certain work files because I’m worried I already messed something up, and then that turns into missing deadlines, which is… not great.\n\nAnd with my certificate classes, I’m behind now too. I keep meaning to catch up, but then I’ll spend forever trying to figure out the “right” order to do things in, and suddenly the night is gone.\n\nI also stopped doing the stuff that used to keep me kind of steady—yoga, running, cooking actual meals. I used to be weirdly proud of my Sunday meal prep containers, like all lined up in the fridge, and now I just… don’t do it. So, yes, I think my worries are making me avoid things, or maybe making everything feel too big to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel extremely tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Yes. By midafternoon at the office I feel like I’ve been unplugged or something. Even answering normal emails feels like this huge task, and sometimes walking to the subway after work feels weirdly hard, like I’m dragging myself.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll just recover this weekend,” but the weekend comes and I rest, or I try to rest, and by Monday I don’t feel like I got my energy back. It’s frustrating because I used to be able to push through a long day better than this.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or having less interest in things you used to enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say sad exactly, or at least I haven’t really stopped to label it that way. Less interest—maybe? But it’s hard to tell because everything feels tangled up with just being behind and tired.\n\nLike, I used to enjoy cooking, but then if I think about cooking now I immediately think about groceries, and money, and whether I bought the right things, and then I remember this one time I made this really good lentil soup—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed a clear “I don’t enjoy anything anymore” feeling. It’s more that I don’t get to things, or I overthink them before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about what other people think of you in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I guess everyone thinks about that sometimes, right? Like if I’m in a meeting and I say something a little awkward, I might replay it later, but then again I also replay emails and, honestly, things like whether I forgot to move my laundry to the dryer, which is not the same thing.\n\nSocial situations… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. Sometimes I’m more focused on just getting through the thing or not seeming scattered, but I can’t tell if that’s about what people think of me specifically or just… everything feeling like it has too many moving parts.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, with other people… sometimes I’m thinking about what to say next, or whether I’m being normal, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nIt also depends who it is. If it’s coworkers, there’s all this meeting stuff and Slack messages and—sorry, I’m drifting. I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern of feeling anxious just because I’m with people. It’s more like I’m already wound up, and then being around people can feel like one more thing to manage.\n\nDoctor: Have your worries or anxiety affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Maybe… not directly with family or friends? I mean, I’ve been less available, I guess. But the bigger thing I notice is everything else is slipping. I’ve started missing work deadlines, which is really not like me, and I’m falling behind in this certificate program I’m doing.\n\nAnd then the things that used to kind of keep me steady—yoga, running, cooking—I just… I’m not doing them. Like I bought vegetables last week thinking I’d cook, and then they just sat there, and then I felt bad about that too. So with people, I’m probably more checked out or harder to make plans with, but it’s all tied into feeling behind on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling worried or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really paid attention to that specifically. Like, I’ll notice I feel wound up, but whether my heart is actually racing or I’m sweating or trembling—I’m not sure.\n\nSometimes I’m more focused on whatever I’m worried about, like the email I didn’t answer or if I’m missing something on my calendar, and then later I realize I was tense, but I don’t really track the physical stuff. I guess I’d have to think about it more.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t know, it’s like my body was asleep but my mind was still working the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you think might have started these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was one single dramatic thing, exactly. It’s more like… it started around when I began my evening graduate certificate program, while I was still working full-time as an analyst. That was about a year and three months ago.\n\nI remember telling myself it would be manageable because classes were in the evening, and I had this whole spreadsheet for work deadlines and tuition and assignments—actually, I make spreadsheets for everything, even groceries sometimes—but yeah, after that started, the sleep problems just kind of became my normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things, not just work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely not just work or school. I mean, that’s where it starts a lot of the time, but then my mind jumps around. Like, I’ll worry I’m falling behind at work, then suddenly I’m thinking maybe I’m wasting all this money on school, and then somehow that turns into, “What if my family thinks I can’t handle my life?”\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it can be some small thing, like one assignment or one bill or even just an email I haven’t answered, and my brain treats it like it’s going to mess up my entire future. Then I’ll try to make a list to calm down, but then the list becomes its own whole thing, and I’m color-coding it, and—sorry, I know that’s not really the point. But yes, it’s everyday stuff too.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, though it’s less like I’m avoiding one specific place and more like I’ve just started dropping things. Like, I’ll put off opening certain work files because I’m worried I already messed something up, and then that turns into missing deadlines, which is… not great.\n\nAnd with my certificate classes, I’m behind now too. I keep meaning to catch up, but then I’ll spend forever trying to figure out the “right” order to do things in, and suddenly the night is gone.\n\nI also stopped doing the stuff that used to keep me kind of steady—yoga, running, cooking actual meals. I used to be weirdly proud of my Sunday meal prep containers, like all lined up in the fridge, and now I just… don’t do it. So, yes, I think my worries are making me avoid things, or maybe making everything feel too big to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel extremely tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Yes. By midafternoon at the office I feel like I’ve been unplugged or something. Even answering normal emails feels like this huge task, and sometimes walking to the subway after work feels weirdly hard, like I’m dragging myself.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll just recover this weekend,” but the weekend comes and I rest, or I try to rest, and by Monday I don’t feel like I got my energy back. It’s frustrating because I used to be able to push through a long day better than this.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or having less interest in things you used to enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say sad exactly, or at least I haven’t really stopped to label it that way. Less interest—maybe? But it’s hard to tell because everything feels tangled up with just being behind and tired.\n\nLike, I used to enjoy cooking, but then if I think about cooking now I immediately think about groceries, and money, and whether I bought the right things, and then I remember this one time I made this really good lentil soup—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed a clear “I don’t enjoy anything anymore” feeling. It’s more that I don’t get to things, or I overthink them before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about what other people think of you in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I guess everyone thinks about that sometimes, right? Like if I’m in a meeting and I say something a little awkward, I might replay it later, but then again I also replay emails and, honestly, things like whether I forgot to move my laundry to the dryer, which is not the same thing.\n\nSocial situations… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. Sometimes I’m more focused on just getting through the thing or not seeming scattered, but I can’t tell if that’s about what people think of me specifically or just… everything feeling like it has too many moving parts.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, with other people… sometimes I’m thinking about what to say next, or whether I’m being normal, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nIt also depends who it is. If it’s coworkers, there’s all this meeting stuff and Slack messages and—sorry, I’m drifting. I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern of feeling anxious just because I’m with people. It’s more like I’m already wound up, and then being around people can feel like one more thing to manage.\n\nDoctor: Have your worries or anxiety affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Maybe… not directly with family or friends? I mean, I’ve been less available, I guess. But the bigger thing I notice is everything else is slipping. I’ve started missing work deadlines, which is really not like me, and I’m falling behind in this certificate program I’m doing.\n\nAnd then the things that used to kind of keep me steady—yoga, running, cooking—I just… I’m not doing them. Like I bought vegetables last week thinking I’d cook, and then they just sat there, and then I felt bad about that too. So with people, I’m probably more checked out or harder to make plans with, but it’s all tied into feeling behind on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling worried or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really paid attention to that specifically. Like, I’ll notice I feel wound up, but whether my heart is actually racing or I’m sweating or trembling—I’m not sure.\n\nSometimes I’m more focused on whatever I’m worried about, like the email I didn’t answer or if I’m missing something on my calendar, and then later I realize I was tense, but I don’t really track the physical stuff. I guess I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your worries have significantly interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. I keep wanting to say “not really,” because I’m still showing up to things, sort of, but I’ve started missing work deadlines, and that’s really not like me. I’m a management analyst, so deadlines are kind of the whole thing.\n\nAnd I’m behind in my certificate classes too, which makes me feel worse because I signed up for them to feel like I was moving forward. Also I’ve stopped doing the stuff that usually keeps me grounded—yoga, running, cooking. I used to be pretty consistent with that, like Sunday meal prep and everything, and now it just feels like too much. So… yeah, daily life definitely. Relationships maybe more indirectly, because I’m less available and more distracted.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

I don’t know, it’s like my body was asleep but my mind was still working the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you think might have started these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was one single dramatic thing, exactly. It’s frustrating because I used to be able to push through a long day better than this.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or having less interest in things you used to enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say sad exactly, or at least I haven’t really stopped to label it that way. Less interest—maybe? It’s more that I don’t get to things, or I overthink them before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about what other people think of you in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think I avoid people specifically because I’m afraid they’ll judge me, but… I do sometimes put off answering texts or emails, and then it becomes this whole thing where I feel awkward because now it’s been too long.

But that might not be the same as being judged. Like, I’ll think about what to say, and then I’ll decide I should answer when I have more time, and then suddenly it’s three days later. I did that with a friend recently—well, not recently, maybe last month—and then I started wondering if she was annoyed, but I don’t know if that’s fear of evaluation or just me overthinking the timing.

So… I guess I don’t really know. I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding social stuff because of judgment, exactly.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s usually not one thing, exactly. It kind of starts with something small—like an email I haven’t answered or a deadline at work—and then my mind jumps to, okay, am I falling behind at work overall? And then it somehow turns into whether I’m wasting money on my certificate classes, or if I’m not doing enough, or if my family is going to think I can’t handle basic adult stuff.

I know that sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels connected. Like one small issue suddenly means my whole future is going off track. Then I’ll remember something totally separate, like tuition or some assignment I didn’t finish, and it all piles together. So… I guess the trigger is often anything that makes me feel like I’m behind or not keeping up.

It’s more that I don’t get to things, or I overthink them before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about what other people think of you in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I guess everyone thinks about that sometimes, right? Like if I’m in a meeting and I say something a little awkward, I might replay it later, but then again I also replay emails and, honestly, things like whether I forgot to move my laundry to the dryer, which is not the same thing.\n\nSocial situations… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. Sometimes I’m more focused on just getting through the thing or not seeming scattered, but I can’t tell if that’s about what people think of me specifically or just… everything feeling like it has too many moving parts.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, with other people… sometimes I’m thinking about what to say next, or whether I’m being normal, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nIt also depends who it is. If it’s coworkers, there’s all this meeting stuff and Slack messages and—sorry, I’m drifting. I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern of feeling anxious just because I’m with people. It’s more like I’m already wound up, and then being around people can feel like one more thing to manage.\n\nDoctor: Have your worries or anxiety affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Maybe… not directly with family or friends? I mean, I’ve been less available, I guess. But the bigger thing I notice is everything else is slipping. I’ve started missing work deadlines, which is really not like me, and I’m falling behind in this certificate program I’m doing.\n\nAnd then the things that used to kind of keep me steady—yoga, running, cooking—I just… I’m not doing them. Like I bought vegetables last week thinking I’d cook, and then they just sat there, and then I felt bad about that too. Like, I’ll notice I feel wound up, but whether my heart is actually racing or I’m sweating or trembling—I’m not sure.\n\nSometimes I’m more focused on whatever I’m worried about, like the email I didn’t answer or if I’m missing something on my calendar, and then later I realize I was tense, but I don’t really track the physical stuff. I guess I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your worries have significantly interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. I keep wanting to say “not really,” because I’m still showing up to things, sort of, but I’ve started missing work deadlines, and that’s really not like me. I’m a management analyst, so deadlines are kind of the whole thing.\n\nAnd I’m behind in my certificate classes too, which makes me feel worse because I signed up for them to feel like I was moving forward. Also I’ve stopped doing the stuff that usually keeps me grounded—yoga, running, cooking. I used to be pretty consistent with that, like Sunday meal prep and everything, and now it just feels like too much. So… yeah, daily life definitely. Relationships maybe more indirectly, because I’m less available and more distracted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say worthless… guilty, maybe, but I don’t know if that’s the right word. Like I’ll think, “Why didn’t I just do that one thing?” and then it turns into this whole mental list.\n\nBut I also feel weird saying guilty because nobody’s, like, accusing me of anything. It’s more like… I’ll look at something I didn’t finish and feel bad about it, and then I remember I also haven’t called someone back, and then I start thinking about how my apartment is kind of a mess, which is embarrassing because I used to be more on top of that. So… I don’t know. Maybe some guilt, but I’m not sure about worthless.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social interactions because you're afraid of being judged or evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think I avoid people specifically because I’m afraid they’ll judge me, but… I do sometimes put off answering texts or emails, and then it becomes this whole thing where I feel awkward because now it’s been too long.\n\nBut that might not be the same as being judged. Like, I’ll think about what to say, and then I’ll decide I should answer when I have more time, and then suddenly it’s three days later. I did that with a friend recently—well, not recently, maybe last month—and then I started wondering if she was annoyed, but I don’t know if that’s fear of evaluation or just me overthinking the timing.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t really know. I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding social stuff because of judgment, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular situation or thing that usually triggers your worries?\n\nPatient: It’s usually not one thing, exactly. It kind of starts with something small—like an email I haven’t answered or a deadline at work—and then my mind jumps to, okay, am I falling behind at work overall? And then it somehow turns into whether I’m wasting money on my certificate classes, or if I’m not doing enough, or if my family is going to think I can’t handle basic adult stuff.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels connected. Like one small issue suddenly means my whole future is going off track. Then I’ll remember something totally separate, like tuition or some assignment I didn’t finish, and it all piles together. So… I guess the trigger is often anything that makes me feel like I’m behind or not keeping up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it is. I keep trying to tell myself it’s just normal stress, but it’s starting to mess with things in a way I can’t really brush off.

Like, I’ve missed work deadlines, which is not really like me—or, it didn’t used to be. And then with my certificate classes, I’m behind there too, and I keep meaning to catch up, but then I sit down and somehow end up reorganizing my notes or checking something unrelated, like meal prep ideas, which is ridiculous because I’m not even cooking much lately.

That’s another thing—I used to have routines that kind of kept me steady, like yoga, running, making actual food instead of just whatever’s easiest. And I’ve kind of stopped doing all of that. So… yes, I guess it’s causing a lot of distress. More than I want to admit.

I don’t know, it’s like my body was asleep but my mind was still working the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you think might have started these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was one single dramatic thing, exactly. That was about a year and three months ago.\n\nI remember telling myself it would be manageable because classes were in the evening, and I had this whole spreadsheet for work deadlines and tuition and assignments—actually, I make spreadsheets for everything, even groceries sometimes—but yeah, after that started, the sleep problems just kind of became my normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things, not just work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely not just work or school. I mean, that’s where it starts a lot of the time, but then my mind jumps around. I keep meaning to catch up, but then I’ll spend forever trying to figure out the “right” order to do things in, and suddenly the night is gone.\n\nI also stopped doing the stuff that used to keep me kind of steady—yoga, running, cooking actual meals. I used to be weirdly proud of my Sunday meal prep containers, like all lined up in the fridge, and now I just… don’t do it. So, yes, I think my worries are making me avoid things, or maybe making everything feel too big to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel extremely tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Yes. By midafternoon at the office I feel like I’ve been unplugged or something. Even answering normal emails feels like this huge task, and sometimes walking to the subway after work feels weirdly hard, like I’m dragging myself.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll just recover this weekend,” but the weekend comes and I rest, or I try to rest, and by Monday I don’t feel like I got my energy back. It’s frustrating because I used to be able to push through a long day better than this.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or having less interest in things you used to enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say sad exactly, or at least I haven’t really stopped to label it that way. Less interest—maybe? But it’s hard to tell because everything feels tangled up with just being behind and tired.\n\nLike, I used to enjoy cooking, but then if I think about cooking now I immediately think about groceries, and money, and whether I bought the right things, and then I remember this one time I made this really good lentil soup—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed a clear “I don’t enjoy anything anymore” feeling. It’s more that I don’t get to things, or I overthink them before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about what other people think of you in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I guess everyone thinks about that sometimes, right? Like if I’m in a meeting and I say something a little awkward, I might replay it later, but then again I also replay emails and, honestly, things like whether I forgot to move my laundry to the dryer, which is not the same thing.\n\nSocial situations… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. Sometimes I’m more focused on just getting through the thing or not seeming scattered, but I can’t tell if that’s about what people think of me specifically or just… everything feeling like it has too many moving parts.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, with other people… sometimes I’m thinking about what to say next, or whether I’m being normal, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nIt also depends who it is. If it’s coworkers, there’s all this meeting stuff and Slack messages and—sorry, I’m drifting. I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern of feeling anxious just because I’m with people. It’s more like I’m already wound up, and then being around people can feel like one more thing to manage.\n\nDoctor: Have your worries or anxiety affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Maybe… not directly with family or friends? I mean, I’ve been less available, I guess. But the bigger thing I notice is everything else is slipping. I’ve started missing work deadlines, which is really not like me, and I’m falling behind in this certificate program I’m doing.\n\nAnd then the things that used to kind of keep me steady—yoga, running, cooking—I just… I’m not doing them. Like I bought vegetables last week thinking I’d cook, and then they just sat there, and then I felt bad about that too. So with people, I’m probably more checked out or harder to make plans with, but it’s all tied into feeling behind on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling worried or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really paid attention to that specifically. Like, I’ll notice I feel wound up, but whether my heart is actually racing or I’m sweating or trembling—I’m not sure.\n\nSometimes I’m more focused on whatever I’m worried about, like the email I didn’t answer or if I’m missing something on my calendar, and then later I realize I was tense, but I don’t really track the physical stuff. I guess I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your worries have significantly interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. I keep wanting to say “not really,” because I’m still showing up to things, sort of, but I’ve started missing work deadlines, and that’s really not like me. I’m a management analyst, so deadlines are kind of the whole thing.\n\nAnd I’m behind in my certificate classes too, which makes me feel worse because I signed up for them to feel like I was moving forward. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… I wouldn’t say sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day. That sounds very specific, and I’m not sure it fits.

I’ll be in bed in my apartment for hours, like I’ve already closed my laptop and I’m technically “done” for the night, but my brain is still going through work deadlines and tuition payments and class assignments, like I’m checking them over and over in my head.\n\nAnd then even when I do sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really turned off. I don’t know, it’s like my body was asleep but my mind was still working the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you think might have started these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was one single dramatic thing, exactly. That was about a year and three months ago.\n\nI remember telling myself it would be manageable because classes were in the evening, and I had this whole spreadsheet for work deadlines and tuition and assignments—actually, I make spreadsheets for everything, even groceries sometimes—but yeah, after that started, the sleep problems just kind of became my normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things, not just work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely not just work or school. I mean, that’s where it starts a lot of the time, but then my mind jumps around. Like, I’ll put off opening certain work files because I’m worried I already messed something up, and then that turns into missing deadlines, which is… not great.\n\nAnd with my certificate classes, I’m behind now too. It’s more that I don’t get to things, or I overthink them before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about what other people think of you in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I guess everyone thinks about that sometimes, right? Like if I’m in a meeting and I say something a little awkward, I might replay it later, but then again I also replay emails and, honestly, things like whether I forgot to move my laundry to the dryer, which is not the same thing.\n\nSocial situations… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. Sometimes I’m more focused on just getting through the thing or not seeming scattered, but I can’t tell if that’s about what people think of me specifically or just… everything feeling like it has too many moving parts.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. Sometimes I’m more focused on just getting through the thing or not seeming scattered, but I can’t tell if that’s about what people think of me specifically or just… everything feeling like it has too many moving parts.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, with other people… sometimes I’m thinking about what to say next, or whether I’m being normal, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nIt also depends who it is. If it’s coworkers, there’s all this meeting stuff and Slack messages and—sorry, I’m drifting. I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern of feeling anxious just because I’m with people. It’s more like I’m already wound up, and then being around people can feel like one more thing to manage.\n\nDoctor: Have your worries or anxiety affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Maybe… not directly with family or friends? I mean, I’ve been less available, I guess. But the bigger thing I notice is everything else is slipping. I’ve started missing work deadlines, which is really not like me, and I’m falling behind in this certificate program I’m doing.\n\nAnd then the things that used to kind of keep me steady—yoga, running, cooking—I just… I’m not doing them. Like I bought vegetables last week thinking I’d cook, and then they just sat there, and then I felt bad about that too. So with people, I’m probably more checked out or harder to make plans with, but it’s all tied into feeling behind on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling worried or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really paid attention to that specifically. Like, I’ll notice I feel wound up, but whether my heart is actually racing or I’m sweating or trembling—I’m not sure.\n\nSometimes I’m more focused on whatever I’m worried about, like the email I didn’t answer or if I’m missing something on my calendar, and then later I realize I was tense, but I don’t really track the physical stuff. I guess I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your worries have significantly interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. I keep wanting to say “not really,” because I’m still showing up to things, sort of, but I’ve started missing work deadlines, and that’s really not like me. I’m a management analyst, so deadlines are kind of the whole thing.\n\nAnd I’m behind in my certificate classes too, which makes me feel worse because I signed up for them to feel like I was moving forward. Also I’ve stopped doing the stuff that usually keeps me grounded—yoga, running, cooking. I used to be pretty consistent with that, like Sunday meal prep and everything, and now it just feels like too much. So… yeah, daily life definitely. Relationships maybe more indirectly, because I’m less available and more distracted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say worthless… guilty, maybe, but I don’t know if that’s the right word. Like I’ll think, “Why didn’t I just do that one thing?” and then it turns into this whole mental list.\n\nBut I also feel weird saying guilty because nobody’s, like, accusing me of anything. It’s more like… I’ll look at something I didn’t finish and feel bad about it, and then I remember I also haven’t called someone back, and then I start thinking about how my apartment is kind of a mess, which is embarrassing because I used to be more on top of that. So… I don’t know. Maybe some guilt, but I’m not sure about worthless.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social interactions because you're afraid of being judged or evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think I avoid people specifically because I’m afraid they’ll judge me, but… I do sometimes put off answering texts or emails, and then it becomes this whole thing where I feel awkward because now it’s been too long.\n\nBut that might not be the same as being judged. Like, I’ll think about what to say, and then I’ll decide I should answer when I have more time, and then suddenly it’s three days later. I did that with a friend recently—well, not recently, maybe last month—and then I started wondering if she was annoyed, but I don’t know if that’s fear of evaluation or just me overthinking the timing.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t really know. I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding social stuff because of judgment, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular situation or thing that usually triggers your worries?\n\nPatient: It’s usually not one thing, exactly. It kind of starts with something small—like an email I haven’t answered or a deadline at work—and then my mind jumps to, okay, am I falling behind at work overall? And then it somehow turns into whether I’m wasting money on my certificate classes, or if I’m not doing enough, or if my family is going to think I can’t handle basic adult stuff.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels connected. Like one small issue suddenly means my whole future is going off track. Then I’ll remember something totally separate, like tuition or some assignment I didn’t finish, and it all piles together. So… I guess the trigger is often anything that makes me feel like I’m behind or not keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I keep trying to tell myself it’s just normal stress, but it’s starting to mess with things in a way I can’t really brush off.\n\nLike, I’ve missed work deadlines, which is not really like me—or, it didn’t used to be. And then with my certificate classes, I’m behind there too, and I keep meaning to catch up, but then I sit down and somehow end up reorganizing my notes or checking something unrelated, like meal prep ideas, which is ridiculous because I’m not even cooking much lately.\n\nThat’s another thing—I used to have routines that kind of kept me steady, like yoga, running, making actual food instead of just whatever’s easiest. And I’ve kind of stopped doing all of that. So… yes, I guess it’s causing a lot of distress. More than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day. That sounds very specific, and I’m not sure it fits.\n\nI mean, I get discouraged, definitely, especially when I think about everything I’m behind on, but then sometimes I’m just distracted or tense more than sad. “Empty” is harder for me to answer—like, I don’t really know what counts as empty versus just being tired of dealing with everything.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of talking around it. I guess my answer is I haven’t really noticed feeling sad or empty like that most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: Not most of the day, I don’t think. I mean, I have moments where I feel down, like when I realize I’ve lost track of something I was supposed to do, but then I get more… worked up or preoccupied than sad, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I’m just not labeling it right, because I’ll be sitting there staring at my laptop and feeling awful, but it’s not like crying-all-day sad. It’s more like my brain is busy making lists and then not doing any of them. So… no, I don’t think I feel sad most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes or stressors in the past year, aside from starting your graduate certificate program?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… aside from the certificate program, I don’t know if there’s been one big “event,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike, nothing jumps out as a major life change. I mean, work has had its usual chaos, but it always has some chaos, and then there’s family stuff here and there, but not like… a clear before-and-after thing. Sometimes I wonder if I’m just bad at noticing stress until it piles up, because I’ll say “no, nothing happened,” and then later remember some random thing that was bothering me for weeks.\n\nBut no, I can’t point to a significant life change besides starting the program.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

That sounds very specific, and I’m not sure it fits.\n\nI mean, I get discouraged, definitely, especially when I think about everything I’m behind on, but then sometimes I’m just distracted or tense more than sad. “Empty” is harder for me to answer—like, I don’t really know what counts as empty versus just being tired of dealing with everything.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of talking around it. I guess my answer is I haven’t really noticed feeling sad or empty like that most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: Not most of the day, I don’t think. I mean, I have moments where I feel down, like when I realize I’ve lost track of something I was supposed to do, but then I get more… worked up or preoccupied than sad, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I’m just not labeling it right, because I’ll be sitting there staring at my laptop and feeling awful, but it’s not like crying-all-day sad. It’s more like my brain is busy making lists and then not doing any of them. So… no, I don’t think I feel sad most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes or stressors in the past year, aside from starting your graduate certificate program?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… aside from the certificate program, I don’t know if there’s been one big “event,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike, nothing jumps out as a major life change. I mean, work has had its usual chaos, but it always has some chaos, and then there’s family stuff here and there, but not like… a clear before-and-after thing. Sometimes I wonder if I’m just bad at noticing stress until it piles up, because I’ll say “no, nothing happened,” and then later remember some random thing that was bothering me for weeks.\n\nBut no, I can’t point to a significant life change besides starting the program.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced a loss of interest in activities that you once enjoyed?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / management_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure. I mean, I still do some things I used to enjoy, but sometimes I don’t get around to them, if that makes sense.

Like, I’ll plan to watch something or meet someone or do something relaxing, and then I end up spending way too long deciding what to do, or I get caught up in some other little task that suddenly feels urgent. Actually, that reminds me, I used to be better about keeping weekends kind of open, but now weekends disappear so fast, and then I’m annoyed because I didn’t use the time well.

But loss of interest… I don’t know if I’d put it that way. I haven’t really noticed that I don’t enjoy things anymore. It’s more that I don’t always get to them.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because my sleep has just kind of fallen apart. I’ll be in bed in my apartment for hours, like I’ve already closed my laptop and I’m technically “done” for the night, but my brain is still going through work deadlines and tuition payments and class assignments, like I’m checking them over and over in my head.\n\nAnd then even when I do sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really turned off. I don’t know, it’s like my body was asleep but my mind was still working the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you think might have started these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was one single dramatic thing, exactly. It’s more like… it started around when I began my evening graduate certificate program, while I was still working full-time as an analyst. That was about a year and three months ago.\n\nI remember telling myself it would be manageable because classes were in the evening, and I had this whole spreadsheet for work deadlines and tuition and assignments—actually, I make spreadsheets for everything, even groceries sometimes—but yeah, after that started, the sleep problems just kind of became my normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things, not just work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely not just work or school. I mean, that’s where it starts a lot of the time, but then my mind jumps around. Like, I’ll worry I’m falling behind at work, then suddenly I’m thinking maybe I’m wasting all this money on school, and then somehow that turns into, “What if my family thinks I can’t handle my life?”\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it can be some small thing, like one assignment or one bill or even just an email I haven’t answered, and my brain treats it like it’s going to mess up my entire future. Then I’ll try to make a list to calm down, but then the list becomes its own whole thing, and I’m color-coding it, and—sorry, I know that’s not really the point. But yes, it’s everyday stuff too.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, though it’s less like I’m avoiding one specific place and more like I’ve just started dropping things. Like, I’ll put off opening certain work files because I’m worried I already messed something up, and then that turns into missing deadlines, which is… not great.\n\nAnd with my certificate classes, I’m behind now too. I keep meaning to catch up, but then I’ll spend forever trying to figure out the “right” order to do things in, and suddenly the night is gone.\n\nI also stopped doing the stuff that used to keep me kind of steady—yoga, running, cooking actual meals. I used to be weirdly proud of my Sunday meal prep containers, like all lined up in the fridge, and now I just… don’t do it. So, yes, I think my worries are making me avoid things, or maybe making everything feel too big to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel extremely tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Yes. By midafternoon at the office I feel like I’ve been unplugged or something. Even answering normal emails feels like this huge task, and sometimes walking to the subway after work feels weirdly hard, like I’m dragging myself.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll just recover this weekend,” but the weekend comes and I rest, or I try to rest, and by Monday I don’t feel like I got my energy back. It’s frustrating because I used to be able to push through a long day better than this.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or having less interest in things you used to enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say sad exactly, or at least I haven’t really stopped to label it that way. Less interest—maybe? But it’s hard to tell because everything feels tangled up with just being behind and tired.\n\nLike, I used to enjoy cooking, but then if I think about cooking now I immediately think about groceries, and money, and whether I bought the right things, and then I remember this one time I made this really good lentil soup—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed a clear “I don’t enjoy anything anymore” feeling. It’s more that I don’t get to things, or I overthink them before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about what other people think of you in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I guess everyone thinks about that sometimes, right? Like if I’m in a meeting and I say something a little awkward, I might replay it later, but then again I also replay emails and, honestly, things like whether I forgot to move my laundry to the dryer, which is not the same thing.\n\nSocial situations… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. Sometimes I’m more focused on just getting through the thing or not seeming scattered, but I can’t tell if that’s about what people think of me specifically or just… everything feeling like it has too many moving parts.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, with other people… sometimes I’m thinking about what to say next, or whether I’m being normal, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nIt also depends who it is. If it’s coworkers, there’s all this meeting stuff and Slack messages and—sorry, I’m drifting. I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern of feeling anxious just because I’m with people. It’s more like I’m already wound up, and then being around people can feel like one more thing to manage.\n\nDoctor: Have your worries or anxiety affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Maybe… not directly with family or friends? I mean, I’ve been less available, I guess. But the bigger thing I notice is everything else is slipping. I’ve started missing work deadlines, which is really not like me, and I’m falling behind in this certificate program I’m doing.\n\nAnd then the things that used to kind of keep me steady—yoga, running, cooking—I just… I’m not doing them. Like I bought vegetables last week thinking I’d cook, and then they just sat there, and then I felt bad about that too. So with people, I’m probably more checked out or harder to make plans with, but it’s all tied into feeling behind on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling worried or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really paid attention to that specifically. Like, I’ll notice I feel wound up, but whether my heart is actually racing or I’m sweating or trembling—I’m not sure.\n\nSometimes I’m more focused on whatever I’m worried about, like the email I didn’t answer or if I’m missing something on my calendar, and then later I realize I was tense, but I don’t really track the physical stuff. I guess I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your worries have significantly interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. I keep wanting to say “not really,” because I’m still showing up to things, sort of, but I’ve started missing work deadlines, and that’s really not like me. I’m a management analyst, so deadlines are kind of the whole thing.\n\nAnd I’m behind in my certificate classes too, which makes me feel worse because I signed up for them to feel like I was moving forward. Also I’ve stopped doing the stuff that usually keeps me grounded—yoga, running, cooking. I used to be pretty consistent with that, like Sunday meal prep and everything, and now it just feels like too much. So… yeah, daily life definitely. Relationships maybe more indirectly, because I’m less available and more distracted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say worthless… guilty, maybe, but I don’t know if that’s the right word. Like I’ll think, “Why didn’t I just do that one thing?” and then it turns into this whole mental list.\n\nBut I also feel weird saying guilty because nobody’s, like, accusing me of anything. It’s more like… I’ll look at something I didn’t finish and feel bad about it, and then I remember I also haven’t called someone back, and then I start thinking about how my apartment is kind of a mess, which is embarrassing because I used to be more on top of that. So… I don’t know. Maybe some guilt, but I’m not sure about worthless.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social interactions because you're afraid of being judged or evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think I avoid people specifically because I’m afraid they’ll judge me, but… I do sometimes put off answering texts or emails, and then it becomes this whole thing where I feel awkward because now it’s been too long.\n\nBut that might not be the same as being judged. Like, I’ll think about what to say, and then I’ll decide I should answer when I have more time, and then suddenly it’s three days later. I did that with a friend recently—well, not recently, maybe last month—and then I started wondering if she was annoyed, but I don’t know if that’s fear of evaluation or just me overthinking the timing.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t really know. I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding social stuff because of judgment, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular situation or thing that usually triggers your worries?\n\nPatient: It’s usually not one thing, exactly. It kind of starts with something small—like an email I haven’t answered or a deadline at work—and then my mind jumps to, okay, am I falling behind at work overall? And then it somehow turns into whether I’m wasting money on my certificate classes, or if I’m not doing enough, or if my family is going to think I can’t handle basic adult stuff.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels connected. Like one small issue suddenly means my whole future is going off track. Then I’ll remember something totally separate, like tuition or some assignment I didn’t finish, and it all piles together. So… I guess the trigger is often anything that makes me feel like I’m behind or not keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I keep trying to tell myself it’s just normal stress, but it’s starting to mess with things in a way I can’t really brush off.\n\nLike, I’ve missed work deadlines, which is not really like me—or, it didn’t used to be. And then with my certificate classes, I’m behind there too, and I keep meaning to catch up, but then I sit down and somehow end up reorganizing my notes or checking something unrelated, like meal prep ideas, which is ridiculous because I’m not even cooking much lately.\n\nThat’s another thing—I used to have routines that kind of kept me steady, like yoga, running, making actual food instead of just whatever’s easiest. And I’ve kind of stopped doing all of that. So… yes, I guess it’s causing a lot of distress. More than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day. That sounds very specific, and I’m not sure it fits.\n\nI mean, I get discouraged, definitely, especially when I think about everything I’m behind on, but then sometimes I’m just distracted or tense more than sad. “Empty” is harder for me to answer—like, I don’t really know what counts as empty versus just being tired of dealing with everything.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of talking around it. I guess my answer is I haven’t really noticed feeling sad or empty like that most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: Not most of the day, I don’t think. I mean, I have moments where I feel down, like when I realize I’ve lost track of something I was supposed to do, but then I get more… worked up or preoccupied than sad, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I’m just not labeling it right, because I’ll be sitting there staring at my laptop and feeling awful, but it’s not like crying-all-day sad. It’s more like my brain is busy making lists and then not doing any of them. So… no, I don’t think I feel sad most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes or stressors in the past year, aside from starting your graduate certificate program?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… aside from the certificate program, I don’t know if there’s been one big “event,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike, nothing jumps out as a major life change. Like one small issue suddenly means my whole future is going off track. Then I’ll remember something totally separate, like tuition or some assignment I didn’t finish, and it all piles together. So… I guess the trigger is often anything that makes me feel like I’m behind or not keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I keep trying to tell myself it’s just normal stress, but it’s starting to mess with things in a way I can’t really brush off.\n\nLike, I’ve missed work deadlines, which is not really like me—or, it didn’t used to be. More than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day. I guess my answer is I haven’t really noticed feeling sad or empty like that most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: Not most of the day, I don’t think. It’s more like my brain is busy making lists and then not doing any of them. So… no, I don’t think I feel sad most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes or stressors in the past year, aside from starting your graduate certificate program?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… aside from the certificate program, I don’t know if there’s been one big “event,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike, nothing jumps out as a major life change.
